Excuse strength All five accounts under credited excuses, the account fails as a whole (Section 8.3). The failure cannot be confined to those materials.

5. Evidence that motivates and challenges closure

>Evidence that motivates and challenges closure

5.1 What existing regularities contribute

No finding in the literature demonstrates closure, though several make it worth looking for. Blame updates in an orderly way as evidence arrives (Monroe & Malle, 2019), and closure predicts that it stays responsive. It can also update, though modestly, when the agent's moral ignorance comes to light after the act (Theisen & Andow, 2026), which the present scheme classes as reinterpretation. Closure alone cannot explain why a negative act should trigger encoding more readily than a positive one. Schema and confirmability accounts largely explain that asymmetry without it (Reeder & Brewer, 1979; Rothbart & Park, 1986). Predictions about later good conduct extend the account beyond its core scope. When the elicitation criterion is met, such conduct should be recorded as a new act, and identity should improve less than an open model calibrated on that conduct predicts. Otherwise it predicts ordinary graded revision. Evidence against this untested extension would leave the core prediction (P1) standing.

5.2 Retraction and reinterpretation

Since closure covers only excuses, work on corrected information bears on the retraction and reinterpretation controls. Retracted misinformation often continues to influence reasoning (Ecker et al., 2022), and that influence appears to depend on whether the reader updates their model of the situation (Hey et al., 2026). For newly formed impressions of persons, however, retracted negative information was often fully discounted, with mixed evidence of residual influence (Mickelberg et al., 2024 (Mickelberg et al., , 2025)). That pattern fits the scope, because a denial changes the encoded act. The influence of corrected false accusations against fictional political candidates nonetheless persisted, regardless of political orientation (Cohen et al., 2024). This is the most direct challenge to the retraction prediction. One reply is that participants may not have accepted the correction, which is why the Context Integration Task measures acceptance directly.

Reinterpretation is on firmer ground. First impressions, including implicit evaluations, can be reversed when the meaning of the original behaviour is recast (Mann & Ferguson, 2015), and such reversals are reported to last (Kurdi et al., 2026). They fit the prediction that reinterpretation revises identity. Only if a pure excuse produced the same reversal would they tell against closure.

An exploratory, non-preregistered reanalysis of both arms of Kim et al. (2022, Study 3), reported in Supplementary Section S2, found that character judgements were revised substantially after a moral dilemma was reframed (+0.59 SD) and after unrelated later good conduct (+0.54 SD). Because those dilemmas already give the "immoral" option a plausible justification, they are classified, after the data were seen and without norming, as likely to fall below the elicitation criterion. The reframing arm, first analysed as a test of mitigation, is likewise reclassified as reinterpretation. The result neither tests nor supports closure, but it indicates that Kim et al.'s character measure is sensitive. Siegel et al. (2018) found that beliefs about bad agents are more uncertain and more responsive than beliefs about good agents, which challenges any general claim that negative moral representations are fixed. It bears less directly on closure, because their participants observed repeated choices, which count here as new acts. A direct comparison would need repeated evidence and a learning model.

5.3 Uncertainty and the true self

Work on the good true self sets a further constraint. People distinguish a person's deep, authentic self from their behaviour and tend to see that self as good (Newman et al., 2015). Essentialist reasoning underlies belief in a true self (Christy et al., 2019), and an essence can be understood as an ideal rather than a hidden cause (Newman & Knobe, 2019). So the theory cannot assume that predictive identity and the evaluative true self differ merely because it names them differently (Section 6).

6. What would distinguish closure from its rivals

Several established accounts produce a flat line without closure. Each curve below is one specified implementation of an account, with auxiliary assumptions. Its family could predict other curves, and a failed curve tells against that implementation alone. Figure 1b plots four alongside closure, and Table 1 summarises eight. Supplementary Section S1.12 specifies each curve and the planned model comparison. No comparison has yet been run.

The serious rival is graded diagnosticity updating, in its person-centred (Uhlmann et al., 2015) and Bayesian forms (Walker et al., 2022). A confident prior or weak likelihood ratios can make its predicted decline in identity shallow. That flexibility makes the comparison difficult, since a prior free to vary could fit almost any flat line. For this reason the likelihoods are elicited independently, and the open model may still estimate small or zero effects. Two comparators belong to the same family, one adapted from inference about hidden situations and capability (Davis et al., 2025), the other from inference about latent structure that allows abrupt classification and slow reversal (Li et al., 2026).

The restrictive schema (Reeder & Brewer, 1979) predicts little discounting until the situation would drive even a moral person to the act. Reeder and Brewer did not specify where that point falls, so the implementation specified here places it at Level 6. The confirmability asymmetry of Rothbart and Park (1986) makes the same prediction for the trait and none for projection to other domains. Capacity or willingness inference (Reeder, 2009) holds that a coerced act shows what the person is able and willing to do, so same-pressure prediction should stay high at Level 6 while unrelated-domain projection falls. In a motive-inference implementation, threat or reluctance changes the motive inferred for yielding even when the immediate aim is fixed (Reeder, 2009; Carlson et al., 2022), so identity can move with inferred motive, whereas closure predicts that it stays flat even when inferred motive improves. Graded diagnosticity updating (Uhlmann et al., 2015; Walker et al., 2022) Near-flat identity under a confident prior or weak likelihood ratios Identity and projection fall across excuse levels wherever elicited likelihoods imply a decline beyond the margin, unlike closure

Restrictive schema (Reeder & Brewer, 1979) Flat identity through moderate excuses Identity and projection step down at Level 6, unlike closure Strict belief perseverance and continued influence (Ross et al., 1975; Ecker et al., 2022; Hey et al., 2026) Flat identity across all excuse levels Identity and projection stay high after both accepted retraction and normed reinterpretation, unlike closure Capacity or willingness inference (Reeder, 2009) Flat identity because a coerced act shows what the person is able and willing to do Unrelated-domain projection falls at Level 6 while same-pressure prediction stays high, unlike closure Motive inference (Reeder, 2009; Carlson et al., 2022) Flat identity while the inferred motive stays unchanged Identity improves as the inferred motive improves, unlike closure Construal failure (Trope & Gaunt, 2000) Flat identity when the excuse is not taken up Causal recognition stays flat across excuse levels, unlike closure Need for cognitive closure (Kruglanski & Webster, 1996) Flat identity among observers high in the need Identity and causal recognition stay flat only among observers high in the need, unlike closure Culpable control (Alicke, 2000) Negative character judgement sustained alongside blame Blame lags causal recognition at Level 6 more in identity-relevant conditions, unlike closure A broad moral trait could also produce the pattern. Present character inferences strongly predict forecasts of future character (White et al., 2020). Unrelated-domain projection is necessary for the identity interpretation, but it is not unique to it. Projection to an unrelated moral domain must exceed projection to ability before it counts as identity-based, because immorality generalises asymmetrically to ability (Mattavelli et al., 2026). Projection resting on an evidencesensitive trait should also weaken as excuses strengthen.

Belief perseverance and continued influence predict the same flat line across excuses as closure. The retraction and reinterpretation arms separate closure from their strict forms, not from partial continued influence. Subtyping protects a broader stereotype by setting disconfirming members apart as exceptions, whereas disconfirmation dispersed across many members weakens it (Weber & Crocker, 1983; Kunda & Oleson, 1995). Applied to one person, subtyping would set the excused act apart as an exception, and a same-target sequence of excuses could separate it from closure.

Construal failure is tested on causal recognition, the need for cognitive closure on moderation of causal recognition and identity by the need, and culpable control on blame. For blame, Figure 1b draws one curve shared by all accounts. A rise in causal recognition tells against construal failure as implemented, although recognising a cause does not show that it was integrated. Culpable control holds that judgements of bad character inflate blame (Alicke, 2000). Its prediction is tested by comparing blame with causal recognition at Level 6 across the identity-relevant and low-diagnosticity conditions.

Any flat rating could also reflect motivated responding, because observers can be reluctant to express reduced condemnation (Geiser et al., 2025). Two features address this possibility, a measure of self-presentation concern taken before the scenario and a format that separates causal explanation from endorsement. A private-versus-accountable manipulation before exposure (Tetlock, 1985) would test it directly and is left to a later extension. The planned comparison will also fit compounds of rivals, and none is assumed to fail.

Predictive identity must also be separated from the true self. Predictive identity supports forecasts across situations. An observer who learns that Alex objected in writing can think better of her true self while still expecting dishonesty from her in other settings. Under closure, true-self ratings should rise with excuse strength while predictive identity stays flat. If true-self ratings stay as flat as predictive identity at Level 6, where the agent is normed as not endorsing the act, the predicted divergence fails, which alone does not make the two constructs identical. If both rise, predictive identity has moved. That is evidence against P1.

7. A hierarchy of testable claims

P1 carries the account, and Table 2 divides the remaining propositions by their role. P2 and P3 test the representational and process interpretations of P1, and P4 to P7 are extensions that need further assumptions (Supplementary Section S3). Support for several extensions cannot compensate for a failure of P1, whose preconditions are set out in Section 8.2. P2 has an experimental form. Conditions normed as identity-relevant and conditions normed as low in diagnosticity should differ more on unrelated-domain projection than on same-pressure prediction. Each difference is standardised on its own latent baseline scale. If that interaction is absent and the identity factor adds nothing to measurement or prediction, a trait-level description is preferable. P3 establishes a discrete transition only if the timing evidence favours a change point over smooth models in which order matters.

8. The Context Integration Task

>The Context Integration Task

8.1 Design

The Context Integration Task presents a violation normed as deliberate, diagnostic and intended to harm, followed by one of six levels of excuse assigned between participants. Level 1 supplies no excuse, and Levels 2 to 5 supply progressively stronger pressure, threat or constraint. Level 6 supplies credible severe coercion with explicit reluctance and non-endorsement. Norming must show that observers do not see the agent as identifying with the act (Woolfolk et al., 2006). P2 Representation Identity measures add information beyond the trait, global evaluation, same-pressure prediction and the evaluative true self.

P3 Process

An excuse has greater influence on identity before encoding than after, beyond calibrated ordinary order effects.

P4 to P7 Extension Deliberation (P4), act valence (P5), domain (P6) and delay (P7) each yield a further prediction that rests on added assumptions (Supplementary Section S3).

Levels 2 to 6 are each normed as an excuse in the sense of Section 3.3. Each participant judges four strangers, one in each of four moral domains (honesty, and the fairness, harm and sanctity foundations of Graham et al., 2013). Two positive-control arms should move identity. In the reinterpretation arm, later information supplies a morally good purpose that norming confirms changes whether the act was wrong. In the retraction arm, it establishes that the allegation was false or wrongly attributed, and a check confirms that participants accepted it. The main further conditions are a no-violation baseline, a low-diagnosticity condition and, to test P3, a condition that gives the excuse before the violation.

Identity and unrelated-domain projection are co-primary outcomes and are measured first. Causal recognition, blame, the domain-specific trait, same-pressure prediction, global evaluation, the evaluative true self and inferred motive are measured separately. So are factual credibility, normative mitigation (whether the information excuses and reduces responsibility), recategorisation (whether it changes wrongness) and forgiveness, since an appeal to who the agent really is may seek forgiveness rather than excuse (Attie-Picker, 2026). The main analyses use no post-treatment selection. Prior positivity, the need for cognitive closure and Trait Judgmentalism are measured before the scenario as covariates and candidate moderators. The formal sequence of Section 3, successive excuses about one person, is tested directly only by a same-target sequence with a comparison group that gives only a final rating (Supplementary Section S3.6). Supplementary Section S1 gives the full specification.

8.2 Decision rule

The test examines the whole trajectory of identity and unrelated-domain projection across the six excuse levels, not an average slope. Across those levels, each may vary by no more than a small margin fixed before confirmatory data are collected (Section 9.1). Every result is classed as invariance, revision or inconclusive. The claim concerns average effects for the declared population of observers and scenarios. Population-general support also requires a scenariolevel criterion, so that opposite-signed changes across scenarios cannot cancel out and mimic invariance. No verdict in either direction is returned unless the norming preconditions hold: the violation produces an identity attribution, each excuse level raises normative mitigation above the level below, and observers do not see the Level 6 agent as identifying with the act. A verdict also requires the uptake check, a rise in causal recognition from Level 1 to Level 6, showing that the strongest excuse was taken up. Any verdict further requires that factual credibility ratings at every level and normative mitigation ratings at Levels 2 to 6 show observers believe the excuse and credit it as mitigating, so causal uptake alone cannot reject the account. A failed blame check, in which blame does not fall, only withholds support. Supplementary Section S1.8 gives the full rule.

8.3 What would reject the account

The critical prediction has two parts, and each part is tested only while the preconditions and the uptake, credibility and mitigation checks of Section 8.2 are met. First, identity and unrelateddomain projection must stay practically invariant across the six excuse levels. Meaningful revision of either, under excuses that observers credit as mitigating, rejects the account outright. Second, in the accepted-retraction arm and in the normed-reinterpretation arm, identity and unrelated-domain projection must each fall below their Level 1 means by more than the prespecified margin. Closure is rejected as the explanation of the dissociation if, in either arm, either construct stays practically invariant or rises by more than that margin. If both arms fail, the pattern favours strict belief perseverance or continued influence. If only the reinterpretation arm fails, the scope rule of Section 3.3 is rejected as well. If only the retraction arm fails, the measure has proved sensitive but the withdrawn accusation persists, so the pattern favours its continued influence (Cohen et al., 2024). An inconclusive result in either part withholds support for closure without rejecting it. A failed construct-separation test under P2, such as a true self that stays as flat as predictive identity when the agent does not endorse the act, does not reject the dissociation. It removes that evidence for a distinct identity construct, and a trait-level description is preferred only if the measurement and incremental prediction tests of Section 7 also favour it.

9. Limitations and implications

>Limitations and implications

9.1 Limitations

The proposal has five serious limitations. (1) No direct test exists, and the public data reanalysed here can neither test nor support the account (Section 5.2). (2) Because a flexible open model contains strict closure as a special case, cross-validated predictive comparison cannot return decisive support for closure even when closure is true. Evidence for closure must come from the trajectory test of Section 8.2. Where open and closed models are indistinguishable, the mechanism is reported as unresolved. (3) The prespecified margin is a fifth of a baseline standard deviation (0.20) for the full range across levels. It is proposed without derivation, and the claim is invariance at that resolution, not an exact zero. (4) The measures are candidate items that require validation for content, response processes, reliability and measurement invariance (Supplementary Section S1.7). (5) Generality across cultures is open. Causal attribution matters less to blame for interdependent observers (Park et al., 2026), and close relationships or group membership may alter both encoding and updating. So may prior trust. These factors should be investigated prospectively and should not be invoked to explain away a failed test.

9.2 Implications

What follows is conditional on the account surviving its test. A test of relabelling would need few new materials. Person-first labels for people who have committed sexual offences softened attitudes towards the category but did not change judgements of named individuals (Harper et al., 2025). Extended beyond excuses, closure predicts that relabelling after encoding changes expressed affect but not identity or unrelated-domain projection, whereas relabelling before encoding reaches identity and projection too (Supplementary Section S3). If closure held beyond one session, an employer given a fuller explanation of an applicant's offence might keep the same expectations of the applicant, and whether such expectations matter for reintegration needs direct tests of contact, hiring and support.

The account is descriptive. On a situationist view of character (Doris, 2002), conduct under strong situational pressure is weak evidence of robust traits, so an observer who accepts Alex's excuse and still judges her a dishonest person would, on that view, be making an error. Doris (2006 Doris ( /2021) ) uses a situationist strategy to argue that the moral psychology underlying the character theory of excuses in legal philosophy is empirically inadequate. Whether closure is such an error or a defensible stance is a separate normative question, which the account informs by specifying which information stops reaching the judgement of the person, and when.

A second normative view points the other way. On Watson's (1996) account, a knowing wrong can disclose what an agent is prepared to do, so an observer who still judges Alex a dishonest person may not be in error, and closure would describe a sound response to the evidence. This view joins two questions that the account keeps apart: whether the judgement of the person is warranted, and whether later mitigation can reach that judgement at all. Even a warranted judgement could respond to an excuse, and a closed judgement could persist without warrant. Alex faced a real but ordinary threat. At Level 6, the agent instead faces credible severe coercion and is normed as not identifying with the act. Watson's own view may still count such a knowing wrong as disclosing what the agent is prepared to do. On the identification reading assumed here, however, attributability should fall there, because an act the agent does not endorse does not express what the agent values, and an observer tracking attributability should think better of the agent. Closure predicts that the identity judgement holds at Level 6 as at the lower levels. If that pattern appears, observers would not be tracking attributability on the identification reading.

10. Conclusion

I have proposed that an accepted excuse can change how an observer explains what Alex did without changing who she is taken to be. On this proposal, the observer would blame her less and still would not trust her with the next set of accounts. The claim concerns reach, and the Context Integration Task could reject it. Section 8.3 states the two results that would count against it. If a model in which excuses still make a small update fits the data as well, the question stays open. An inconclusive result would reject nothing. If closure holds beyond a single session, a full explanation of what someone did may leave unchanged what others expect of them in settings such as hiring or parole.

Figure 1. Update rule and qualitative predicted shapes. (a) Updates permitted within a declared episode. The violation V produces the identity representation E 0 and the occasion record O 0 , and each excuse C k updates the occasion record.
